Dynamics of two interacting kinks for the ϕ 6 \phi^{6} model
Abdon Moutinho

Abstract
We consider the nonlinear wave equation known as the ϕ 6 \phi^{6} model in dimension 1+1. We describe the long time behavior of all the solutions of this model close to a sum of two kinks with energy slightly larger than twice the minimum energy of non constant stationary solutions. We prove orbital stability of two moving kinks. We show for low energy excess ϵ \epsilon that these solutions can be described for long time less or equivalent than − ln ⁡ ( ϵ ) ​ ϵ − 1 2 -\ln{(\epsilon)}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}} as the sum of two moving kinks such that each kink’s center is close to an explicit function which is a solution of an ordinary differential system. We give an optimal estimate in the energy norm of the remainder ( g ⁡ ( t ) , ∂ t g ⁡ ( t ) ) (g(t),\partial_{t}g(t)) and we prove that this estimate is achieved during a finite instant t = T ≲ − ln ⁡ ( ϵ ) ​ ϵ − 1 2 . t=T\lesssim-\ln{(\epsilon)}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}.



1
Introduction

We consider a nonlinear wave equation equation known as the
\phi^{6}
model. For the potential function
U(\phi)=\phi^{2}(1-\phi^{2})^{2}
and
\dot{U}(\phi)=2\phi-8\phi^{3}+6\phi^{5},
the equation is written as

\partial_{t}^{2}\phi(t,x)-\partial_{x}^{2}\phi(t,x)+\dot{U}(\phi(t,x))=0\text{, $(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}$}.

(1)

The potential energy
E_{pot},
the kinetic energy
E_{kin}
and total energy
E_{total}
associated to the equation (
1
) are given by

\displaystyle E_{pot}(\phi(t))=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}\phi(t,x)^{2}\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(t,x)^{2}(1-\phi(t,x)^{2})^{2}\,dx,

\displaystyle E_{kin}(\phi(t))=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)^{2}\,dx,

\displaystyle E_{total}(\phi(t),\partial_{t}\phi(t))=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\partial_{x}\phi(t,x)^{2}+\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)^{2}\right]\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(t,x)^{2}(1-\phi(t,x)^{2})^{2}\,dx.

We say that if a solution
\phi(t,x)
of the integral equation associated to (
1
) has
E_{total}(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)<+\infty,
then it is in the energy space. The solutions of (
1
) in the energy space have constant total energy
E_{total}(\phi(t),\partial_{t}\phi(t)).

By standard arguments, the Cauchy Problem associated (
1
) is locally well-posed in the energy space, moreover is globally well-posed since
U(\phi)=\phi^{2}(1-\phi^{2})^{2}
satisfies
\lim_{|\phi|\to\infty}U(\phi)=+\infty.

The stationary solutions of (
1
) are the critical points of the potential energy. The only non-constant stationary solutions in (
1
) are the topological solitons called kinks and anti-kinks, for more details see chapter
5
of
[
21
]
. The kinks of (
1
) are given by

H_{0,1}(x-a)=\frac{e^{\sqrt{2}(x-a)}}{(1+e^{2\sqrt{2}(x-a)})^{\frac{1}{2}}},\,H_{-1,0}(x-a)=-H_{0,1}(-x+a)

for any real
a.
The study of kink and multi kinks solitons solutions of nonlinear wave equations has applications in many domains of mathematical physics. More precisely, the model (
1
) that we study has applications in condensed matter physics
[
2
]
and cosmology
[
32
]
,
[
13
]
,
[
10
]
.

It is well known that the set of solutions in energy Space of (
1
) for any potential
U
is invariant under space translation, time translation and space reflection. Also, for one of the stationary solutions
H
of (
1
) and any
-1<v<1
, we have that the following solitary wave

H\Big(\frac{x-vt}{(1-v^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big),

which is the Lorentz transform of
H
is a solution of (
1
).

The problem of stability of multi-kinks is of great interest in mathematical physics, see for example
[
7
]
,
[
9
]
. For the integrable model
mKdV
, Muñoz proved in
[
24
]
the
H^{1}
stability and asymptotic stability of multi-kinks. However, for many non-integrable models as the
\phi^{6}
nonlinear wave equation, the asymptotic and long time dynamics of multi-kinks after the instant where the collision or interaction happens are still unknown, even though there are numerical studies of kink-kink collision for the
\phi^{6}
model, see
[
9
]
, which motivate our research on the topic of the description of long time behavior of a kink-kink pair.

For nonlinear wave equation models in
1+1
space dimension, results of stability for a single kink were obtained, for example for the
\phi^{4}
model it was obtained asymptotic stability for odd pertubations in
[
17
]
and
[
6
]
. Also, it was recently proved in
[
18
]
by Martel, Muñoz, Kowalczyk and Van Den Bosch asymptotic stability of a single kink for a general class of nonlinear wave equations, including the model which we study here.

The main purpose of our material is to describe the long time behavior of solutions
\phi(t,x)
of (
1
) in the energy space such that

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to+\infty}\phi(t,x)=1,

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to-\infty}\phi(t,x)=-1

with total energy equals to
2E_{pot}(H_{01})+\epsilon,
for
0<\epsilon\ll 1.
More precisely, we proved orbital stability for a sum of two moving kinks with total total energy
2E_{pot}(H_{0,1})+\epsilon
and we verified that the remainder has a better estimate during a long time interval which goes to
\mathbb{R}
as
\epsilon\to 0,
indeed we proved that the estimate of the remainder during this long time interval is optimal. Also, we prove that the dynamics of the kinks movement is very close to two explicit functions
d_{j}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
defined in Theorem
1.11
during a long time interval. This result is very important to understand the behavior of two kinks after the instant of collision, which happens when the kinetic energy is minimal, indeed, our main results Theorem
1.7
and Theorem
1.11
describe the dynamics of the kinks before and after the collision instant for a long time interval. The numerical study of interaction and collision between kinks for the
\phi^{6}
model was done in
[
9
]
, in which it was verified that the collision of kinks is close to an elastic collision when the speed of each kink is low and smaller than a critical speed
v_{c}
.

For nonlinear wave equation models in dimension
2+1,
there are similar results obtained in the dynamics of topological multi-solitons. For the Higgs Model, there are results in the description dynamics of multi-vortices in
[
28
]
obtained by Stuart and in
[
12
]
obtained by Gustafson and Sigal. Indeed, we took inspiration from the proof and statement of Theorem
2
of
[
12
]
to construct our main results. Also, in
[
29
]
, Stuart described the dynamics of monopole solutions for the Yang-Mills-Higgs equation. For more references, see also
[
30
]
,
[
8
]
,
[
20
]
and
[
11
]
.

In
[
1
]
, Bethuel, Orlandi and Smets described the asymptotic behavior of solutions of a parabolic Ginzburg-Landau equation closed to multi-vortices in the initial instant. For mores references, see also
[
15
]
and
[
27
]
.

There are also results in the dynamics in multi-vortices for nonlinear Schrödinger equation, for example the description of the dynamics of multi-vortices for the Gross-Pitaevski equation was obtained in
[
25
]
by Ovchinnikov and Sigal and results in the dynamics of vortices for the Ginzburg-Landau-Schrödinger equations were proved in
[
5
]
by Colliander and Jerrard, see also
[
16
]
for more information about Gross-Pitaevski equation.

1.1
Main results

We recall that the objective of this paper is to show orbital stability for the solutions of the equation (
1
) which are close to a sum of two interacting kinks in an initial instant and estimate the size of the time interval where better stability properties hold. The main techniques of the proof are modulation techniques adapted from
[
19
]
,
[
23
]
and
[
26
]
and a refined energy estimate method to control the size of the remainder term.

Notation 1.1
.

For any
D\subset\mathbb{R},
any real function
f:D\subset\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},
a real positive function
g
with domain
D
is in
O(f(x))
if and only if there is a uniform constant
C>0
such that
0<g(x)<C|f(x)|.
We denote that two real non-negative functions
f,g:D\subset\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
satisfy

f\lesssim g,

if there is a constant
C>0
such that

\left\lvert f(x)\right\rvert\leq C\left\lvert g(x)\right\rvert\text{, for all $x\in D.$}

If
f\lesssim g
and
g\lesssim f,
we denote that
f\cong g.

We use the notation
(x)_{+}\coloneqq\max(x,0)
.
If
g(t,x)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R},L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))\cap C(\mathbb{R},H^{1}(\mathbb{R})),

then we define
\overrightarrow{g(t)}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by

\overrightarrow{g(t)}=(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t)),

and we also denote the energy norm of the remainder
\overrightarrow{g(t)}
as

\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert=\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}+\left\lVert\partial_{t}g(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

to simplify our notation in the text.

Definition 1.2
.

We define
S
as the set
g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that

1.

\frac{dg}{dx}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

2.

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\left\lvert g(x)-1\right\rvert^{2}\,dx<\infty,

3.

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{<0}}\left\lvert g(x)+1\right\rvert^{2}\,dx<\infty.

The partial differential equation (
1
) is locally well-posed in the affine space
S\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Motivated by the proof and computations that we are going to present, we also consider:

Definition 1.3
.

We define for
x_{1},\,x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}

H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}(x)\coloneqq H_{0,1}(x-x_{2})
and
H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}(x)\coloneqq H_{-1,0}(x-x_{1}),

and we say that
x_{2}
is the kink center of
H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}(x)
and
x_{1}
is the kink center of
H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}(x).

Remark 1.4
.

Indeed,
S=\{g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})|\,g-H_{0,1}-H_{-1,0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\}.

There are also non-stationary solutions
(\phi(t,x),\partial_{t}\phi(t,x))
of (
1
) with finite total energy
E_{total}(\phi(t),\partial_{t}\phi(t))
that satisfies for all
t\in\mathbb{R}

\lim_{x\to+\infty}\phi(t,x)=1,\,\lim_{x\to-\infty}\phi(t,x)=0.

(2)

But, for any
a\in\mathbb{R},
the kinks
H_{0,1}(x-a)
are the unique functions that minimize the Potential Energy in the set of functions
\phi(x)
satisfying condition (
2
), the proof of this fact follows from the Bogomolny identity, see
[
21
]
or section
2
of
[
19
]
. By a similar reasoning, we can verify that all functions
\phi(x)\in S
have
E_{pot}(\phi)>2E_{pot}(H_{0,1}).

Definition 1.5
.

We define the energy excess
\epsilon
of a solution
(\phi(t),\partial_{t}\phi(t))\in S\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as the following value

\epsilon=E_{total}(\phi(t),\partial_{t}\phi(t))-2E_{pot}(H_{0,1}).

Also, for
\phi(t)
solution of (
1
), we denote the Kinetic Energy of
\phi(t)
by
E_{kin}(\phi(t))=E(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)-E_{pot}(\phi(t))
.
We recall the notation
(x)_{+}\coloneqq\max(x,0)
. It’s not difficult to verify the following inequalities

(D1)

\left\lvert H_{0,1}(x)\right\rvert\leq e^{-\sqrt{2}(-x)_{+}},

(D2)

\left\lvert H_{-1,0}(x)\right\rvert\leq e^{-\sqrt{2}(x)_{+}},

(D3)

\left\lvert\dot{H}_{0,1}(x)\right\rvert\leq\sqrt{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}(-x)_{+}},

(D4)

\left\lvert\dot{H}_{-1,0}(x)\right\rvert\leq\sqrt{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}(x)_{+}}.

Moreover, since

\ddot{H}_{0,1}(x)=\dot{U}(H_{0,1}(x)),

(3)

we can verify by induction the following estimate

\left\lvert\frac{d^{k}H_{0,1}(x)}{dx^{k}}\right\rvert\lesssim_{k}\min\Big(e^{-2\sqrt{2}x},\,e^{\sqrt{2}x}\Big)

(4)

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}.

The following result is crucial in the framework of this material:

Lemma 1.6

(Modulation Lemma)
.

\exists\,C_{0}\,,\delta_{0}>0
, such that if
0<\delta\leq\delta_{0}
,
x_{2},\,x_{1}
are real numbers with
x_{2}-x_{1}\geq\frac{1}{\delta}
and
g\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\leq\delta
, then for
\phi(x)=H_{-1,0}(x-x_{1})+H_{0,1}(x-x_{2})+g(x)
,
\exists!\,y_{1},\,y_{2}
such that for

g_{1}(x)=\phi(x)-H_{-1,0}(x-y_{1})-H_{0,1}(x-y_{2}),

the four following statements are true

1

\langle g_{1},\,\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}(x-y_{1})\rangle_{L^{2}}=0,

2

\langle g_{1},\,\partial_{x}H_{0,1}(x-y_{2})\rangle_{L^{2}}=0,

3

\left\lVert g_{1}\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C_{0}\delta,

4

\left\lvert y_{2}-x_{2}\right\rvert+\left\lvert y_{1}-x_{1}\right\rvert\leq C_{0}\delta.

We will refer the first and second statements as the orthogonality conditions of the Modulation Lemma.

Proof.

See the Appendix section
A
.
∎

Now, our main results are the following:

Theorem 1.7
.

\exists\,C,\delta_{0}>0
, such that if
\epsilon<\delta_{0}
and

(\phi(0),\partial_{t}\phi(0))\in S\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

with
E_{total}(\phi(0),\partial_{t}\phi(0))=2E_{pot}(H_{0,1})+\epsilon
, then there are
x_{2},x_{1}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
functions such that the unique global time solution
\phi(t,x)
of (
1
) is given by

\phi(t)=H_{0,1}(x-x_{2}(t))+H_{-1,0}(x-x_{1}(t))+g(t),

(5)

with
g(t)
satisfying orthogonality conditions of the Modulation Lemma and

e^{-\sqrt{2}(x_{2}(t)-x_{1}(t))}+\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\left\lvert\ddot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert+\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\dot{x}_{j}(t)^{2}+\left\lVert(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t))\right\rVert_{H^{1}\times L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\epsilon.

Furthermore, we have that

\left\lVert(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t))\right\rVert_{H^{1}\times L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\Big[\left\lVert(g(0),\partial_{t}g(0))\right\rVert_{H^{1}\times L^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\Big]\exp\Big(\frac{C\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|}{\ln{(\frac{1}{\epsilon})}}\Big)\text{ for all $t\in\mathbb{R}.$}

(6)

Remark 1.8
.

In notation of the statement of Theorem
1.7
, for any
\delta>0,
there is
0<K(\delta)<1
such that if
0<\epsilon<K(\delta),

E_{total}(\phi(0),\partial_{t}\phi(0))=2E_{pot}(H_{0,1})+\epsilon,
then we have that
\left\lVert(g(0),\partial_{t}g(0))\right\rVert_{H^{1}\times L^{2}}<\delta
and
x_{2}(0)-x_{1}(0)>\frac{1}{\delta},
for the proof see Lemma
A.3
and Corollary
A.4
in the Appendix section
A
.

Remark 1.9

(Optimal decay.)
.

The result of Theorem
1.7
is optimal in the sense that for any function

r:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}

with
\lim_{h\to 0}r(h)=0,
there is a positive value
\delta(r)
such that if
0<\epsilon<\delta(r)
and
\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(0)}\right\rVert\leq r(\epsilon)\epsilon,
then

\epsilon\lesssim\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert
for some
0<t=O\Big(\frac{\ln{(\frac{1}{\epsilon})}}{\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big).

The proof of this fact is in the Appendix section
B
.

Remark 1.10
.

From Remark
1.9
, we obtain that there is an
0<\delta_{0}
such that if
0<\epsilon<\delta_{0},
then for any
(\phi(0,x),\partial_{t}\phi(0,x))\in S\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
E_{total}(\phi(0),\partial_{t}\phi(0))
equals to
2E_{pot}(H_{0,1})+\epsilon,\,g(t,x)
defined in identity (
5
) satisfies

\epsilon\lesssim\limsup\limits_{t\to+\infty}\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert,
similarly we have that
\epsilon\lesssim\limsup\limits_{t\to-\infty}\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert.

The proof of this fact is in the Appendix section
B
.

Theorem 1.11
.

\exists C,\delta_{0}>0,
such that if
0<\epsilon<\delta_{0},\,(\phi(0),\partial_{t}\phi(0))\in S\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
and
E_{total}(\phi(0),\partial_{t}\phi(0))=2E_{pot}(H_{0,1})+\epsilon,

then there are
v_{1},\,v_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\begin{pmatrix}\phi(0)\\
\partial_{t}\phi(0)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}H_{0,1}(x-x_{2}(0))+H_{-1,0}(x-x_{1}(0))+g_{0}(x)\\
v_{2}\partial_{x}H_{0,1}(x-x_{2}(0))+v_{1}\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}(x-x_{1}(0))+g_{1}(x)\end{pmatrix}

with
g_{0}
satisfying the orthogonality conditions of Modulation Lemma

\displaystyle\left\langle\dot{H}_{0,1}(x-x_{2}(0)),\,g_{1}(x)\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=-v_{2}\left\langle\ddot{H}_{0,1}(x-x_{2}(0)),\,g_{0}(x)\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

\displaystyle\left\langle\dot{H}_{-1,0}(x-x_{1}(0)),\,g_{1}(x)\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=-v_{1}\left\langle\ddot{H}_{-1,0}(x-x_{1}(0)),\,g_{0}(x)\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

and
\epsilon
the energy excess of the solution
(\phi(t,x),\partial_{t}\phi(t,x))
of (
1
). Indeed, let the smooth functions
d_{1}(t),\,d_{2}(t)
be defined by

\displaystyle d_{1}(t)=a+bt-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\ln{\Big(\frac{8}{v^{2}}\cosh{\big(\sqrt{2}vt+c\big)}^{2}\Big)},

(7)

\displaystyle d_{2}(t)=a+bt+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\ln{\Big(\frac{8}{v^{2}}\cosh{\big(\sqrt{2}vt+c\big)}^{2}\Big)},

(8)

such that
d_{j}(0)=x_{j}(0),\,\dot{d}_{j}(0)=-v_{j}
for
j\in\{1,\,2\}.
Let
d(t)=d_{2}(t)-d_{1}(t),
then, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}

\left\lvert z(t)-d(t)\right\rvert\lesssim\min(\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert t\right\rvert,\epsilon t^{2}),\,\left\lvert\dot{z}(t)-\dot{d}(t)\right\rvert\lesssim\epsilon\left\lvert t\right\rvert,

moreover, we have the following estimates

\displaystyle\epsilon\max_{j\in\{1,\,2\}}\left\lvert d_{j}(t)-x_{j}(t)\right\rvert=O\left(\max\Big(\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(0)}\right\rVert,\epsilon\Big)^{2}\ln{\Big(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big)}^{11}\exp\Big(\frac{C\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert t\right\rvert}{\ln{(\frac{1}{\epsilon})}}\Big)\right),

(9)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\max_{j\in\{1,\,2\}}\left\lvert\dot{d}_{j}(t)-\dot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert=O\left(\max\Big(\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(0)}\right\rVert,\epsilon\Big)^{2}\ln{\Big(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big)}^{11}\exp\Big(\frac{C\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert t\right\rvert}{\ln{(\frac{1}{\epsilon})}}\Big)\right).

(10)

Remark 1.12
.

The proof of Theorem
1.7
and Theorem
1.11
for
t\leq 0
is analogous to the proof for
t\geq 0,
so we will only prove them for
t\geq 0.

Theorem
1.7
will be obtained as a consequence of Theorem
1.11
.
Clearly, from Theorem
1.11
, we can deduce the following corollary.

Corollary 1.13
.

With the same hypotheses as in Theorem
1.11
, we have that

\max_{j\in\{1,\,2\}}|\ddot{d}_{j}(t)-\ddot{x}_{j}(t)|=O\left(\max\Big(\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(0)}\right\rVert,\epsilon\Big)\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\exp\Big(\frac{C\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert t\right\rvert}{\ln{(\frac{1}{\epsilon})}}\Big)+\max\Big(\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(0)}\right\rVert,\epsilon\Big)^{2}\ln{\Big(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big)}^{11}\exp\Big(\frac{C\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert t\right\rvert}{\ln{(\frac{1}{\epsilon})}}\Big)\right).

Proof of Corollary
1.13
.

It follows directly from Theorem
1.11
and from Lemma
A.1
presented in the Appendix Section
A
.
∎

1.2
Resume of the proof

In this subsection, we present how the article is organized and explain briefly the content of each section.

Section 2.
In this section, we prove orbital stability of a perturbation of a sum of two kinks. Moreover, we prove that if the initial data
(\phi(0,x),\partial_{t}\phi(0,x))
satisfies the hypothesis of Theorem
1.7
, then there are real functions
x_{1},\,x_{2}
of class
C^{2}
such that for all
t\geq 0

\displaystyle\left\lVert\phi(t,x)-H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}-H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\left\lVert\partial_{t}\left(\phi(t,x)-H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}-H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The proof of the orbital stability follows from studying the expression

E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+g)-E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}),

which is bigger than
\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert^{2}
less some remaining terms from Taylor’s Expansion Theorem and the fact that the kinks are critical points of
E_{pot}.
But, from the modulation lemma, we will introduce the functions
x_{2},\,x_{1}
that will guarantee the following coercitivity property

\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert^{2}\lesssim E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+g)-E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}).

From the orthogonality conditions of the modulation lemma and standard ordinary differential equation techniques, we also obtain uniform bounds for
\left\lVert\dot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})},\,\left\lVert\ddot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}
for
j\in\{1,\,2\}.
The main techniques of this section are an adaption of section 2 and 3 of
[
19
]
.

Section 3.

In this section, we study the long time behavior of
\dot{x}_{j}(t),\,x_{j}(t)
for
j\in\{1,\,2\}.
More precisely, we elaborate a Lemma similar to the Lemma
3.5
of
[
19
]
, but our estimates are more precise, more precisely the errors of our estimate are written in function of
z(t),\,\dot{x}_{j}(t),\,\dot{x}_{j}(t)
and
\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert.

Section 4.

In Section
4,
we introduce a functional
F(t)
with the objective of controlling
\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert
for a long time interval. More precisely, we show that the function
F(t)
satisfies for a constant
K>0
the global estimate
\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert^{2}\lesssim F(t)+K\epsilon^{2}
and we show that
|\dot{F}(t)|
is small enough for a long time interval. We start the functional from the quadratic part of the total energy of
\phi(t),
more precisely with

D(t)=\left\lVert\partial_{t}g(t,x)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\left\lVert\partial_{x}g(t,x)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ddot{U}(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)+H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x))g(t,x)^{2}\,dx.

However, we obtain that the terms of worst decay that appear in the computation of
\dot{D}(t)
are expressions similar to

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}g(t,x)F(x_{1},x_{2},\dot{x}_{1},\dot{x}_{2},x)\,dx.

But, we can cancel these bad terms after we add to the functional
D(t)
correction terms similar to

-\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(t,x)F(x_{1},x_{2},\dot{x}_{1},\dot{x}_{2},x)\,dx,

and now in the time derivative of
D(t)
plus the correction terms, we obtain an expression with size smaller or equivalent to

\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert\left\lVert\partial_{t}(F(x_{1},x_{2},\dot{x}_{1},\dot{x}_{2},x))\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R})}\max_{j\in{1,2}}\left\lvert\dot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert.

Finally, based on the correction term described in the proof of Lemma 4.2 of
[
19
]
, we aggregate another kind of correction term such that its time derivative cancels with

-\int_{\mathbb{R}}U^{(3)}(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)+H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x))(\dot{x}_{2}(t)\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}+\dot{x}_{1}(t)\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0})g(t,x)^{2},

and then we evaluate the time derivative of the functional obtained from this sum D(t) with all the corrections terms.

Remaining Sections.
In the remaining part of this paper, we prove our main results, Theorem
1.7
is a consequence of the energy estimate obtained in Section 4 and the estimates with higher precision of the modulations parameters
x_{1}(t),\,x_{2}(t)
which are obtained in Section
5.
In Section 5, we prove the result of Theorem
1.11
, where we study the evolution of the precision of the modulation parameters estimates by comparing it with a solution of a system of ordinary differential equations. Complementary information are given in Appendices
A
and
B
.

2
Global Stability of two moving kinks

Before the presentation of the proof of the main theorem, we define a functional to study the potential energy of a sum of two kinks.

Definition 2.1
.

The function
A:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

A(z)\coloneqq E_{pot}(H_{0,1}^{z}(x)+H_{-1,0}(x)).

(11)

The study of the function
A
is essential to obtain global in time control of the norm of the remainder
g
and the lower bound of
x_{2}(t)-x_{1}(t)
in Theorem
1.7
.

Remark 2.2
.

It’s easy to verify that

E_{pot}(H_{0,1}(x-x_{2})+H_{-1,0}(x-x_{1}))=E_{pot}(H_{0,1}(x-(x_{2}-x_{1}))+H_{-1,0}(x)).

We will use several times the following elementary estimate from the Lemma 2.5 of
[
19
]
given by:

Lemma 2.3
.

For any real numbers
x_{2},x_{1}
, such that
x_{2}-x_{1}>0
and
\alpha,\,\beta>0
with
\alpha\neq\beta
the following bound holds:

\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\alpha(x-x_{1})_{+}}e^{-\beta(x_{2}-x)_{+}}\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}e^{-\min(\alpha,\beta)(x_{2}-x_{1})},

For any
\alpha>0
, the following bound holds

\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\alpha(x-x_{1})_{+}}e^{-\alpha(x_{2}-x)_{+}}\lesssim_{\alpha}(1+(x_{2}-x_{1}))e^{-\alpha(x_{2}-x_{1})}.

The main result of this section is the following

Lemma 2.4
.

The function
A
is of class
C^{2}
and there is a constant
C>0
, such that

1.

\left\lvert\ddot{A}(z)-4\sqrt{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}z}\right\rvert\leq Cze^{-2\sqrt{2}z},

2.

\left\lvert\dot{A}(z)+4e^{-\sqrt{2}z}\right\rvert\leq Cze^{-2\sqrt{2}z},

3.

\left\lvert A(z)-2E_{pot}(H_{0,1})-2\sqrt{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}z}\right\rvert\leq Cze^{-2\sqrt{2}z}.

Proof.

By definition of
A
, it’s clear that

\displaystyle A(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big(\partial_{x}\big[H^{z}_{0,1}(x)+H_{-1,0}(x)\big]\Big)^{2}\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}}U(H^{z}_{0,1}(x)+H_{-1,0}(x))\,dx

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}H^{z}_{0,1}(x)\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}(x)\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}}U(H^{z}_{0,1}(x)+H_{-1,0}(x))\,dx.

Since the functions
U
and
H_{0,1}
are smooth and
\partial_{x}H_{0,1}(x)
has exponential decay when
|x|\to+\infty
, it’s possible to differentiate
A(z)
in
z.
More precisely, we obtain

\displaystyle\dot{A}(z)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial^{2}_{x}H^{z}_{0,1}(x)\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}(x)\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\dot{U}(H^{z}_{0,1}(x)+H_{-1,0}(x))\partial_{x}H^{z}_{0,1}(x)\,dx

(12)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}H^{z}_{0,1}(x)\big[\dot{U}(H_{-1,0})(x)-\dot{U}(H_{-1,0}(x)+H^{z}_{0,1}(x))\big]\,dx.

(13)

By similar reasons, it is always possible to differentiate
A(z)
twice, precisely, we obtain

\ddot{A}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}H^{z}_{0,1}(x)^{2}\ddot{U}(H_{-1,0}(x)+H_{0,1}^{z}(x))-\partial^{2}_{x}H^{z}_{0,1}(x)\big[\dot{U}(H_{-1,0}(x))-\dot{U}\big(H_{-1,0}(x)+H^{z}_{0,1}(x))\big]\,dx.

Then, integrating by parts, we obtain

\ddot{A}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}H^{z}_{0,1}(x)\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}(x)\big[\ddot{U}(H_{-1,0}(x))-\ddot{U}(H_{-1,0}(x)+H^{z}_{0,1}(x))\big]\,dx.

(14)

Now, consider the function

B(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}H_{0,1}(x)\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}(x+z)\big[\ddot{U}(0)-\ddot{U}(H_{0,1}(x))\big]\,dx.

(15)

Then, we have

\left\lvert\ddot{A}(z)-B(z)\right\rvert=\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}H^{z}_{0,1}(x)\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}(x)\big[\big(\ddot{U}(H_{-1,0}(x))-\ddot{U}(H_{-1,0}(x)+H^{z}_{0,1}(x))\big)-\big(\ddot{U}(0)-\ddot{U}(H_{0,1}^{z}(x))\big)\big]\,dx\right\rvert.

(16)

Also, it’s not difficult to verify the following identity

\big[\ddot{U}(H_{-1,0}(x))-\ddot{U}(H_{-1,0}(x)+H^{z}_{0,1}(x))\big]-\big[\ddot{U}(0)-\ddot{U}(H^{z}_{0,1}(x))\big]=-\int_{0}^{H_{-1,0}(x)}\int_{0}^{H_{0,1}^{z}(x)}U^{(4)}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\,d\omega_{1}\,d\omega_{2}.

(17)

So, the identities (
17
) and (
16
) imply the following inequality

\left\lvert\ddot{A}(z)-B(z)\right\rvert\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\lvert\partial_{x}H^{z}_{0,1}(x)\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}(x)\right\rvert\left\lvert\int_{0}^{H_{-1,0}(x)}\int_{0}^{H^{z}_{0,1}(x)}|U^{(4)}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\,d\omega_{1}\,d\omega_{2}\right\rvert\,dx.

(18)

Since
U
is smooth and
\left\lVert H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}=1
, we have that there is a constant
C>0
such that

\left\lvert\ddot{A}(z)-B(z)\right\rvert\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\lvert\partial_{x}H^{z}_{0,1}(x)\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}(x)H_{-1,0}(x)H^{z}_{0,1}(x)\right\rvert\,dx.

(19)

Now, Using the inequalities from (D1) to (D4) and Lemma
2.3
to the above inequality (
19
), we obtain that exist a constant
C_{1}
non dependent of
z
such that

\left\lvert\ddot{A}(z)-B(z)\right\rvert\leq C_{1}ze^{-2\sqrt{2}z}.

(20)

Also, it’s not difficult to verify that the estimate

\left\lvert\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}(x)-\sqrt{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}x}\right\rvert\leq C\min(e^{-3\sqrt{2}x},e^{-\sqrt{2}x}).

(21)

and the identity (
15
) imply the inequality

\left\lvert B(z)-\sqrt{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}z}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\sqrt{2}x}\partial_{x}H_{0,1}(x)(\ddot{U}(0)-\ddot{U}(H_{0,1}(x)))\,dx\right\rvert\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\lvert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}(x)\right\rvert\min\big(e^{-3\sqrt{2}(x+z)},e^{-\sqrt{2}(x+z)}\big)\,dx\\
\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2\sqrt{2}(-x)_{+}}\min\big(e^{-3\sqrt{2}(x+z)},e^{-\sqrt{2}(x+z)}\big)\,dx\lesssim\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{-2\sqrt{2}(z-x)_{+}}e^{-\sqrt{2}x}\,dx+\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-2\sqrt{2}(z-x)_{+}}e^{-3\sqrt{2}(x)_{+}}\,dx.

(22)

Since, we have the following identity and an estimate from Lemma
2.3

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{-2\sqrt{2}(z-x)}e^{-\sqrt{2}x}\,dx=\frac{e^{-2\sqrt{2}z}}{\sqrt{2}},

(23)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-2\sqrt{2}(z-x)_{+}}e^{-3\sqrt{2}(x)_{+}}\lesssim e^{-2\sqrt{2}z},

(24)

we obtain, then:

\left\lvert B(z)-\sqrt{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}z}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\sqrt{2}x}\partial_{x}H_{0,1}(x)\big[\ddot{U}(0)-\ddot{U}(H_{0,1}(x))\big]\,dx\right\rvert\lesssim e^{-2\sqrt{2}z},

(25)

which clearly implies with (
20
) the inequality

\left\lvert\ddot{A}(z)-\sqrt{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}z}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\sqrt{2}x}\partial_{x}H_{0,1}(x)\big[\ddot{U}(0)-\ddot{U}(H_{0,1}(x))\big]\,dx\right\rvert\lesssim ze^{-2\sqrt{2}z}.

(26)

Also we have the identity

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(8(H_{0,1}(x))^{3}-6(H_{0,1}(x))^{5}\big)e^{-\sqrt{2}x}\,dx=2\sqrt{2},

(27)

for the proof consult the Appendix
A
.
Also, since we have the identity
\ddot{U}(0)-\ddot{U}(\phi)=24\phi^{2}-30\phi^{4}
, by integration by parts, we obtain

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{e^{-\sqrt{2}x}}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_{x}H_{0,1}(x)\big[\ddot{U}(0)-\ddot{U}(H_{0,1}(x))\big]\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(8(H_{0,1}(x))^{3}-6(H_{0,1}(x))^{5}\big)e^{-\sqrt{2}x}\,dx.

(28)

In conclusion, inequality (
26
) is equivalent to
\Big|\ddot{A}(z)-4\sqrt{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}z}\Big|\lesssim ze^{-2\sqrt{2}z}.
The identities

\displaystyle\dot{U}(\phi)+\dot{U}(\theta)-\dot{U}(\phi+\theta)=24\phi\theta(\phi+\theta)-6\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{4}\begin{pmatrix}5\\
j\end{pmatrix}\phi^{j}\omega^{5-j}\Big),

\displaystyle\dot{A}(z)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}H^{z}_{0,1}(x)\big[\dot{U}(H^{z}_{0,1}(x)+H_{-1,0}(x))+\dot{U}(H_{-1,0}(x))-\dot{U}(H^{z}_{0,1}(x))\big]\,dx

and Lemma
2.3
imply the following estimate for
z>0

\left\lvert\dot{A}(z)\right\rvert\lesssim e^{-\sqrt{2}z},

so
\lim_{|z|\to+\infty}\left\lvert\dot{A}(z)\right\rvert=0
. In conclusion, integrating inequality
\left\lvert\ddot{A}(z)-4\sqrt{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}z}\right\rvert\lesssim ze^{-2\sqrt{2}z}
from
z
to
+\infty
we obtain the second result of the lemma

\left\lvert\dot{A}(z)+4e^{-\sqrt{2}z}\right\rvert\lesssim ze^{-2\sqrt{2}z}.

(29)

Finally, from the fact that
\lim_{z\to+\infty}E_{pot}(H_{-1,0}+H^{z}_{0,1}(x))=2E_{pot}(H_{0,1})
, we obtain the last estimate integrating inequality (
29
) from
z
to
+\infty
, which is

\left\lvert 2E_{pot}(H_{0,1})+2\sqrt{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}z}-A(z)\right\rvert\lesssim ze^{-2\sqrt{2}z}.

∎

It is not difficult to verify that the Fréchet derivative of
E_{pot}
as a linear functional from
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
to
\mathbb{R}
is given by

(DE_{pot}(\phi))(v)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}\phi(x)\partial_{x}v(x)+\dot{U}(\phi(x))v(x)\,dx.

(30)

Also, it is not difficult to verify that for any
v,\,w\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, we have

\left\langle D^{2}E_{pot}(\phi)v,\,w\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}v(x)\partial_{x}w(x)\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ddot{U}(\phi(x))v(x)w(x)\,dx.

(31)

Lemma 2.5

(Coercitivity Lemma)
.

\exists\,C,c,\,\delta>0,
such that if
x_{2}-x_{1}\geq\frac{1}{\delta}
, then for any
g\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
we have

\left\langle D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})g,\,g\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\geq c\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}-C\left[\langle g,\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}\rangle^{2}+\langle g,\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}\rangle^{2}\right].

(32)

The proof of this lemma is based in the proof of Lemma 2.4 from
[
19
]
. To prove the Coercitivity Lemma, we need the following result about the spectrum and kernel of the operators
D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}),\,D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}).

Lemma 2.6
.

The operators
D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}),\,D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1})
satisfy the following properties

1

\ker D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})=\left\{c\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0},\,c\in\mathbb{C}\right\},\,\ker D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1})=\left\{c\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}}_{0,1},\,c\in\mathbb{C}\right\}

2

\sigma(D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}))=\sigma(D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}))\subset\{0\}\cup[\lambda_{1},+\infty)
, with
\lambda_{1}>0.

Proof.

Since the operators
D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})
and
D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})
are equivalent by reflection and translation, they have the same spectrum that
D^{2}E_{pot}(H_{-1,0}).
So, we’ll just analyse the spectrum of the operator

D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})=-\partial^{2}_{x}+\ddot{U}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}).

(33)

Also, we will only study the kernel of
D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}),
since the kernel of the other operator can be found by similar reasoning.

If we derive the Bogomolny equation satisfied by
H_{-1,0}

\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}(x)=\dot{U}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}(x))

(34)

with respect to
x,
we obtain the identity

\partial^{2}_{x}(\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}(x))=\ddot{U}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}(x))\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}(x),

(35)

which implies that
\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}\in\ker D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})
. Also,
\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}(x)\neq 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, so the Sturm-Liouville Oscillation Theory implies indeed that
0
is the minimum element of the discrete spectrum of
D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})
and

\ker D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})=\left\{c\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0},\,c\in\mathbb{C}\right\}.

(36)

In conclusion, we have obtained that for some constant
\lambda_{1}>0

\sigma_{d}(D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}))\subset\{0\}\cup[\lambda_{1},+\infty).

(37)

By similar reasoning, we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{d}(D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}))\subset\{0\}\cup[\lambda_{1},+\infty),

(38)

\displaystyle\ker(D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}))=\left\{c\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}}_{0,1},\,c\in\mathbb{C}\right\}.

(39)

Now, it remains to estimate the lower bound of the essential spectrum of both operators. The main tool used to estimate the essential spectrum is a theorem of Spectral Theory written in the book
[
3
]
.

Theorem 2.7
.

Suppose
A
and
B
are self-adjoint operators on a Hilbert Space
\mathcal{H}
. If
\exists z\in\mathbb{C}
, such that
(A-z)^{-1}-(B-z)^{-1}
is compact, then
\sigma_{ess}(A)=\sigma_{ess}(B)
.

Since
D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})=-\partial^{2}_{x}+(2-24(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})^{2}+30(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})^{4})
, we can rewrite this operator as

D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})=\Big(-\partial^{2}_{x}+\big[2-24(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})^{2}+30(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})^{4}-2\chi_{[0,+\infty)}(x)-8\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(x)\big]\\
+\big[2\chi_{[0,+\infty)}(x)+8\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(x)\big]\Big).

Now, we consider

T_{1}=-\partial^{2}_{x}+\big[2\chi_{[0,+\infty)}(x)+8\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(x)\big].

The next step is to check that for the self-adjoint operators
A=D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})
,
B=T_{1}
and for
z=-i
all the hypothesis of Theorem
2.7
are fulfilled, which would imply that
\sigma_{ess}(D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}))=\sigma_{ess}(T_{1}).

Since we have the identity

(D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})+i)^{-1}-(T_{1}+i)^{-1}=-(D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})+i)^{-1}\circ\Big(2-24(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})^{2}\\
+30(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})^{4}-2\chi_{[0,+\infty)}(x)-8\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(x)\Big)\circ(T_{1}+i)^{-1},

(40)

to prove that
D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})
and
T_{1}
have same essential spectrum, we only need to verify that

T_{2}=\Big(2-24(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})^{2}+30(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})^{4}-2\chi_{[0,+\infty)}(x)-8\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(x)\Big)\circ(T_{1}+i)^{-1}

(41)

is a compact operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. By asymptotic properties of
H_{-1,0}
, it is not difficult to verify that

Y=\Big(2-24(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})^{2}+30(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})^{4}-2\chi_{[0,+\infty)}(x)-8\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(x)\Big)

decays exponentially when
\left\lvert x\right\rvert
goes to
+\infty
. Also, it is not difficult to verify that
(T_{1}+i)^{-1}
is a bounded map from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. The last information and the Banach-Alaoglu Theorem imply that for any bounded sequence
(v_{n})\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\exists\,w\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and a subsequence that for simplicity we’ll still denote by
(v_{n})
such that

(T_{1}+i)^{-1}(v_{n})\underset{H^{1}}{\rightharpoonup}w.

(42)

Also, from the fact that
(T_{1}+i)^{-1}(v_{n})
is uniformly bounded in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, it can be verified that for any compact interval
K\subset\mathbb{R}
that

(T_{1}+i)^{-1}(v_{n})\underset{L^{\infty}(K)}{\rightarrow}w,

and this fact with the exponential decay of
Y
and
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
implies directly the following convergence

T_{2}(v_{n})\underset{L^{2}}{\rightarrow}Y(w),

(43)

which implies that
T_{1}
and
D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})
have the same essential spectrum, more precisely

\sigma_{ess}\left(D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})\right)\subset[2,+\infty),

(44)

and so,

\sigma_{ess}\left(D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1})\right)\subset[2,+\infty).

(45)

This finishes the proof of Lemma
2.6
.
∎

Before starting the demonstration of the Coercitivity Lemma, let’s consider from now on the function
0\leq\zeta\leq 1
to be a smooth function satisfying:

\zeta(x)=\begin{cases}1,\text{ if $x\leq\frac{3}{4}$,}\\
0,\text{ if $x\geq\frac{4}{5}$.}\end{cases}

(46)

Proof of Coercitivity Lemma.

Our proof follows the scheme of proof of Lemma
2.4
of
[
19
]
. Here we denote
\langle,\,\rangle
to be the scalar product on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. First, because of Lemma
2.6
, there is a
\lambda>0
such that for any
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})

\left\langle D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})v,\,v\right\rangle\geq\lambda(\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}-\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{-2}\langle v,\,\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}^{x_{1}}\rangle^{2}).

(47)

Also, because of the identity (
33
), we have

\left\langle D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})v,\,v\right\rangle=\left\lVert\partial_{x}v\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ddot{U}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}(x))(v(x))^{2}\,dx.

(48)

Then, inequalities (
47
) and (
48
) imply for any
0<\theta<1
that

\left\langle D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})v,\,v\right\rangle\geq\theta\lambda\big[\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}-\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{-2}\langle v,\,\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}^{x_{1}}\rangle^{2}\big]+(1-\theta)\big[\left\lVert\partial_{x}v\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\\
+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ddot{U}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}(x))(v(x))^{2}\,dx\big].

(49)

Since
\left\lVert\ddot{U}(H_{-1,0}(x))\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}<\infty
, we can choose
\theta
close enough to 1, and obtain from (
49
) the following inequality for a positive constant
c>0
such that

\left\langle D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})v,\,v\right\rangle\geq c\big[\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}-\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{-2}\langle v,\,\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}^{x_{1}}\rangle^{2}\big].

(50)

By similar reasoning, we also have

\langle D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1})v,\,v\rangle\geq c\big[\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}-\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{-2}\langle v,\,\partial_{x}H_{0,1}^{x_{2}}\rangle^{2}\big].

(51)

Now to study the operator
D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})
, consider the function
\zeta_{1}(x)=\zeta(\frac{x-x_{1}}{x_{2}-x_{1}})
and also

\displaystyle V\coloneqq(\ddot{U}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}}_{0,1})-\ddot{U}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1})),

(52)

\displaystyle D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})=-\partial^{2}_{x}+\ddot{U}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1})+V.

(53)

It can be verified that the support of
(1-\zeta_{1}(x))
is included in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}|\,\frac{x-x_{1}}{x_{2}-x_{1}}\geq\frac{3}{4}\}
, and so

\displaystyle(1-\zeta_{1}(x))\left\lvert H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}(x)\right\rvert\leq e^{-\frac{3\sqrt{2}(x_{2}-x_{1})}{4}},

(54)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\langle V(x)(1-\zeta_{1}(x))v(x),\,(1-\zeta_{1}(x))v(x)\rangle\right\rvert\lesssim e^{-\frac{3\sqrt{2}(x_{2}-x_{1})}{4}}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(55)

Therefore, if
\delta>0
is small enough, from
x_{2}-x_{1}\geq\frac{1}{\delta}
, we obtain from the inequality (
55
) that

\left\lvert\langle V(x)(1-\zeta_{1}(x))v(x),\,(1-\zeta_{1}(x))v(x)\rangle\right\rvert\leq O(\delta)\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2},

(56)

so the inequalities (
56
) and (
51
) imply for a
c>0

\left\langle D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}}_{0,1})((1-\zeta_{1}(x))v),\,(1-\zeta_{1}(x))v\right\rangle\geq c\big[\left\lVert(1-\zeta_{1})v\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}-\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{-2}\langle(1-\zeta_{1})v,\,\partial_{x}H_{0,1}^{x_{2}}\rangle^{2}\big]\\
-O(\delta)\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

(57)

Also the support of
\zeta_{1}(x)
is included
\{x|\,\frac{x-x_{2}}{x_{2}-x_{1}}\leq{-\frac{1}{5}}\}.
So, by similar arguments, we can verify the analogous inequality

\left\langle D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}}_{0,1})(\zeta_{1}(x)v),\,\zeta_{1}(x)v\right\rangle\geq c\big[\left\lVert\zeta_{1}v\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}-\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{-2}\langle\zeta_{1}v,\,\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}^{x_{1}}\rangle^{2}\big]-O(\delta)\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

(58)

Also, we obtain that there is a uniform constant
C>0
such that if
\delta>0
is small enough, then we obtain the following estimate for all
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})

\left\langle\partial_{x}(\zeta_{1}(x)v(x)),\,\partial_{x}\left[(1-\zeta_{1}(x))v(x)\right]\right\rangle\geq-C\delta\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

(59)

Also, if
\delta>0
is small enough, we have that
\ddot{U}(H_{-1,0}(x))>1
for
\frac{3(x_{2}-x_{1})}{4}\leq x-x_{1}\leq\frac{4(x_{2}-x_{1})}{5}
. In conclusion, since the support of
(1-\zeta_{1}(x))\zeta_{1}(x)
is included in
\{x-x_{1}\in[\frac{3(x_{2}-x_{1})}{4},\,\frac{4(x_{2}-x_{1})}{5}]\}
, we have the following inequality

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ddot{U}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}(x))\zeta_{1}(x)(1-\zeta_{1}(x))(v(x))^{2}\,dx\geq 0.

(60)

Finally, from the mean value theorem, the knowledge of the support of
\zeta_{1}
and the exponential decay of
H_{0,1}(x)
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big[\ddot{U}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}(x)+H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}(x))-\ddot{U}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}(x))\big]\zeta_{1}(x)(1-\zeta_{1}(x))v(x)^{2}\,dx\right\rvert\leq o(1)\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(61)

Therefore, the inequalities (
59
), (
60
) and (
61
) imply for a uniform constant
C>0
that

\langle D^{2}E_{pot}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}}_{0,1})(\zeta_{1}v),\,(1-\zeta_{1})v\rangle\geq-C\delta\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

(62)

Since we know that support of
\zeta_{1}
is included in
\{x|\,\frac{x-x_{2}}{x_{2}-x_{1}}\leq{-\frac{1}{5}}\}
, we can deduce the estimate

\left\lvert\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}}_{0,1},\,(1-\zeta_{1})v\rangle^{2}-\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}}_{0,1},\,v\rangle^{2}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}}_{0,1},\,\zeta_{1}v\rangle\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}}_{0,1},\,(2-\zeta_{1})v\rangle\right\rvert\leq O(\delta)\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2},

(63)

and similarly,

\left\lvert\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0},\,\zeta_{1}v\rangle^{2}-\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0},\,v\rangle^{2}\right\rvert\leq O(\delta)\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(64)

Therefore, we have that (
57
), (
58
), (
62
), (
63
) and (
64
) imply the inequality (
32
) of the statement.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

There is a constant
C_{2}
, such that if
x_{2}-x_{1}>0
, then

\left\lVert DE_{pot}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C_{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}(x_{2}-x_{1})}.

(65)

Proof.

By the definition of the potential energy, the equation (
3
) and the exponential decay of the two kinks functions, we have that

DE_{pot}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})=\dot{U}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})-\dot{U}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1})-\dot{U}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})

as a bounded linear operator from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
\mathbb{C}
. So, we have that

DE_{pot}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})=-24H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}\big[H^{x_{2}}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0}\big]+6\Big[\sum_{j=1}^{4}\begin{pmatrix}5\\
j\end{pmatrix}(H^{x_{1}}_{-1,0})^{j}(H^{x_{2}}_{0,1})^{5-j}\Big],

and, then, the conclusion follows directly from Lemma
2.3
,
(D1)
and
(D2).

∎

Theorem 2.9

(Orbital Stability of a sum of two moving kinks)
.

\exists\delta_{0}>0
such that if the solution
\phi
of (
1
) satisfies
(\phi(0),\partial_{t}\phi(0))\in S\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and the energy excess
\epsilon=E(\phi)-2E_{pot}(H_{0,1})
is smaller than
\delta_{0}
, then
\exists x_{1},\,x_{2}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
functions of class
C^{2}
, such that for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
denoting
g(t)=\phi(t)-H_{0,1}(x-x_{2}(t))+H_{-1,0}(x-x_{1}(t))
and
z(t)=x_{2}(t)-x_{1}(t)
, we have:

1.

\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}=O(\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}),

2.

z(t)\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[\ln{(\frac{1}{\epsilon})}+\ln{2}],

3.

\left\lVert\partial_{t}\phi(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq 2\epsilon,

4.

\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}|\dot{x}_{j}(t)|^{2}+\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}|\ddot{x}_{j}(t)|=O(\epsilon).

Proof.

First, from the fact that
E_{total}(\phi(x))>2E_{pot}(H_{0,1}),
we deduce, from the conservation of total energy, the estimate

\left\lVert\partial_{t}\phi(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq 2\epsilon.

(66)

From Remark
1.8
, we can assume if
\epsilon\ll 1
that there are
p_{1},\,p_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\phi(0,x)=H_{0,1}(x-p_{2})+H_{-1,0}(x-p_{1})+g_{1}(x),

such that

\left\lVert g_{1}\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}<\delta,\,p_{2}-p_{1}>\frac{1}{\delta},

for a small constant
\delta>0
.
Since the equation
1
is locally well-posed in the space
S\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
we conclude that there is a
\delta_{1}>0
depending only on
\delta
and
\epsilon
such that if
-\delta_{1}\leq t\leq\delta_{1},
then

\left\lVert\phi(t,x)-H_{0,1}(x-p_{2})-H_{-1,0}(x-p_{1})\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq 2\delta.

(67)

If
\delta,\epsilon>0
are small enough, then, from the inequality (
67
) and the Modulation Lemma, we obtain in the time interval
[-\delta_{1},\delta_{1}]
the existence of modulations parameters
x_{1}(t),\,x_{2}(t)
such that for

g(t)=\phi(t)-H_{0,1}(x-x_{2}(t))-H_{-1,0}(x-x_{1}(t)),

we have

\displaystyle\left\langle g(t),\,\partial_{x}H_{0,1}(x-x_{2}(t))\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\left\langle g(t),\,\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}(x-x_{1}(t))\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=0,

(68)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left\lvert x_{2}(t)-x_{1}(t)\right\rvert}+\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\delta.

(69)

From now on, we denote
z(t)=x_{2}(t)-x_{1}(t).
From the Energy Conservation Law, we have for
-\delta_{1}\leq t\leq\delta_{1}
that

E(\phi(t))=2E_{pot}(H_{0,1})+\epsilon=\frac{\left\lVert\partial_{t}\phi(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}{2}+E_{pot}\big(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\big)+\big\langle DE_{pot}\big(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\big),\,g(t)\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\\
+\frac{\big\langle D^{2}E_{pot}\big(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\big)g(t),\,g(t)\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}{2}+O(\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{3}).

From Lemma
2.4
and (
69
), the above identity implies that

\epsilon=\frac{\left\lVert\partial_{t}\phi(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}{2}+2\sqrt{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}+\big\langle DE_{pot}\big(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\big),\,g(t)\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\frac{\big\langle D^{2}E_{pot}\big(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\big)g(t),\,g(t)\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}{2}\\
+O\Big(\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{3}+z(t)e^{-2\sqrt{2}z(t)}\Big)

(70)

for
-\delta_{1}\leq t\leq\delta_{1}.

From (
65
), it is not difficult to verify that

\left\lvert\langle DE_{pot}(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}+H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}),\,g(t)\rangle\right\rvert\leq C_{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}.

So, the equation (
70
) and the Coercitivity Lemma imply, while
-\delta_{1}\leq t\leq\delta_{1}
, the following inequality

\epsilon+C_{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\geq\frac{\left\lVert\partial_{t}\phi(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2}+2\sqrt{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}+\frac{c\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}{2}+O\big(\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{3}+z(t)e^{-2\sqrt{2}z(t)}\big).

(71)

Finally, applying the Young Inequality in the term
C_{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}
, we obtain that the inequality (
71
) can be rewritten in the form

\epsilon\geq\frac{\left\lVert\partial_{t}\phi(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2}+2\sqrt{2}e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}+\frac{c\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}{4}+O\big(\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{3}+z(t)e^{-2\sqrt{2}z(t)}+e^{-2\sqrt{2}z(t)}\big).

(72)

Then, the estimates (
72
), (
69
) imply for
\delta>0
small enough the following inequality

\epsilon\geq\frac{\left\lVert\partial_{t}\phi(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2}+2e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}+\frac{c\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}{8}.

(73)

So, the inequality (
73
) implies the estimates

\displaystyle e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}<\frac{\epsilon}{2},

(74)

\displaystyle\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\lesssim\epsilon,

(75)

for
t\in[-\delta_{1},\delta_{1}]
. In conclusion, if
\frac{1}{\delta}\lesssim\ln{(\frac{1}{\epsilon})}^{\frac{1}{2}},
we can conclude by a bootstrap argument that
the inequalities (
66
), (
74
), (
75
) are true for all
t\in\mathbb{R}.

More precisely, we study the set

C=\left\{b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}|\,\epsilon\geq\frac{\left\lVert\partial_{t}\phi(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2}+2e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}+\frac{c\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}{8}\text{, if $\left\lvert t\right\rvert\leq b.$}\right\}

and prove that
M=\sup_{b\in C}b=+\infty.
We already have checked that
C
is not empty, also
C
is closed by its definition. Now from the previous argument, we can verify that the set where inequality (
73
) holds is open. So, by connectivity, we obtain that
C=\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

In conclusion, it remains to prove that the modulation parameters
x_{1}(t),\,x_{2}(t)
are of class
C^{2}
and that the fourth item of the statement of Theorem
2.9
is true.

(Proof of the
C^{2}
regularity of
x_{1},\,x_{2},
and of the fourth item.)

For
\delta_{0}>0
small enough, we denote
(y_{1}(t),\,y_{2}(t))
to be the solution of the following system of ordinary differential equations, with the function
g_{1}(t)=\phi(t,x)-H^{y_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)-H^{y_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)
,

\Big(\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}-\left\langle g_{1}(t),\,\partial^{2}_{x}H^{y_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle\Big)\dot{y}_{1}(t)+\Big(\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{y_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial_{x}H^{y_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle\Big)\dot{y}_{2}(t)=-\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{y_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)\right\rangle,

(76)

\Big(\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{y_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial_{x}H^{y_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle\Big)\dot{y}_{1}(t)+\Big(\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}-\left\langle g_{1}(t),\,\partial^{2}_{x}H^{y_{2}}_{0,1}\right\rangle\Big)\dot{y}_{2}(t)=-\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{y_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)\right\rangle,

(77)

with initial condition
(y_{2}(0),y_{1}(0))=(x_{2}(0),x_{1}(0))
.
This ordinary differential equation system is motivated from the time derivative of the orthogonality conditions of the Modulation Lemma.

Since we have the estimate
\ln{(\frac{1}{\epsilon})}\lesssim x_{2}(0)-x_{1}(0)
and
g_{1}(0)=g(0),
Lemma
2.3
and the inequality (
75
) imply that the matrix

\begin{bmatrix}\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}-\left\langle g_{1}(0),\,\partial^{2}_{x}H^{y_{1}(0)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle&\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{y_{2}(0)}_{0,1},\,\partial_{x}H^{y_{1}(0)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle\\
\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{y_{2}(0)}_{0,1},\,\partial_{x}H^{y_{1}(0)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle&\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}-\left\langle g_{1}(0),\,\partial^{2}_{x}H^{y_{2}}_{0,1}\right\rangle\end{bmatrix}

(78)

is positive, so we have from Picard-Lindelöf Theorem that
(y_{2}(t),y_{1}(t))
are of class
C^{1}
for some interval
[-\delta,\delta],
with
\delta>0
depending on
\left\lvert x_{2}(0)-x_{1}(0)\right\rvert
and
\epsilon.
From the fact that
(y_{2}(0),y_{1}(0))=(x_{2}(0),x_{1}(0)),
we obtain, from the equations (
76
) and (
77
), that
(y_{2}(t),y_{1}(t))
also satisfies the orthogonality conditions of Modulation Lemma for
t\in[-\delta,\delta].
In conclusion, the uniqueness of Modulation Lemma implies that
(y_{2}(t),y_{1}(t))=(x_{2}(t),x_{1}(t))
for
t\in[-\delta,\delta].
From this argument, we also have for
t\in[-\delta,\delta]
that

e^{-\sqrt{2}(y_{2}(t)-y_{1}(t))}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2\sqrt{2}}.

By bootstrap, we can show, repeating the argument above, that

\sup\left\{C>0|\,(y_{2}(t),y_{1}(t))=(x_{2}(t),x_{1}(t)),\text{for $t\in[-C,C]$}\right\}=+\infty.

(79)

Also, the argument above implies that if
(y_{1}(t),y_{2}(t))=(x_{1}(t),x_{2}(t))
in an instant
t,
then
y_{1},\,y_{2}
are of class
C^{1}
in a neighborhood of
t.
In conclusion,
x_{1},\,x_{2}
are functions in
C^{1}(\mathbb{R}).

Finally, since
\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}=O(\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}=O(\epsilon),
the following matrix

M(t)\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}-\left\langle g(t),\,\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle&\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle\\
\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle&\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}-\left\langle g(t),\,\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right\rangle\end{bmatrix}

(80)

is uniformly positive for all
t\in\mathbb{R}.
So, from the estimate
\left\lVert\partial_{t}\phi(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=O(\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}),
the identities
x_{j}(t)=y_{j}(t)
for
j=1,2
and the equations (
76
) and (
77
), we obtain

\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\left\lvert\dot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert=O(\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(81)

Since the matrix
M(t)
is invertible for any
t\in\mathbb{R},
we can obtain from the equations (
76
), (
77
) that the functions
\dot{x}_{1}(t),\dot{x}_{2}(t)
are given by

\begin{bmatrix}\dot{x}_{1}(t)\\
\dot{x}_{2}(t)\end{bmatrix}=M(t)^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}-\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)\right\rangle\\
-\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)\right\rangle\end{bmatrix}.

(82)

Now, since we have that
(\phi(t),\partial_{t}\phi(t))\in C(\mathbb{R},S\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
and
x_{1}(t),\,x_{2}(t)
are of class
C^{1},
we can deduce that
(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t))\in C(\mathbb{R},H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})).
So, by definition, we can verify that
M(t)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{4}).

Also, since
\phi(t,x)
is the solution in distributional sense of (
1
), we have that for any
y_{1},\,y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
the following identities hold

\displaystyle\big\langle\partial_{x}H^{y_{2}}_{0,1},\,\partial^{2}_{t}\phi(t)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\partial_{x}H^{y_{2}}_{0,1},\,\partial^{2}_{x}\phi(t)-\dot{U}(\phi(t))\big\rangle=-\big\langle\partial^{2}_{x}H^{y_{2}}_{0,1},\,\partial_{x}\phi(t)\big\rangle-\big\langle\partial_{x}H^{y_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\dot{U}(\phi(t))\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\big\langle\partial_{x}H^{y_{1}}_{-1,0},\,\partial^{2}_{t}\phi(t)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\partial_{x}H^{y_{1}}_{-1,0},\,\partial^{2}_{x}\phi(t)-\dot{U}(\phi(t))\big\rangle=-\big\langle\partial^{2}_{x}H^{y_{1}}_{-1,0},\,\partial_{x}\phi(t)\big\rangle-\big\langle\partial_{x}H^{y_{1}}_{-1,0},\,\dot{U}(\phi(t))\big\rangle.

Since (
1
) is locally well-posed in
S\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
we obtain from the identities above that the following functions
h(t,y)\coloneqq\big\langle\partial_{x}H^{y}_{0,1},\,\partial^{2}_{t}\phi(t)\big\rangle
and
l(t,y)\coloneqq\big\langle\partial_{x}H^{y}_{-1,0},\,\partial^{2}_{t}\phi(t)\big\rangle
are continuous in the domain
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}.

So, from the continuity of the functions
h(t,y),\,l(t,y)
and from the fact that
x_{1},\,x_{2}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}),
we obtain that the functions

h_{1}(t)\coloneqq-\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)\rangle,\,h_{2}(t)\coloneqq-\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)\rangle

are of class
C^{1}.
In conclusion, from the equation (
82
), by chain rule and product rule,
we verify that
x_{1},\,x_{2}
are in
C^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Now, since
x_{1},\,x_{2}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\dot{x}_{1},\,\dot{x}_{2}
satisfy (
82
), we deduce after derive at time the function

M(t)\begin{bmatrix}\dot{x}_{1}(t)\\
\dot{x}_{2}(t)\end{bmatrix},

the following equations

\ddot{x}_{1}(t)\Big(\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\langle\partial_{x}g(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle\Big)+\ddot{x}_{2}(t)\Big(\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right\rangle\Big)=\dot{x}_{1}(t)^{2}\Big(\left\langle\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial_{x}g(t)\right\rangle\Big)\\
+\dot{x}_{1}(t)\left\langle\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial_{t}g(t)\right\rangle+\dot{x}_{1}(t)\dot{x}_{2}(t)\left\langle\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right\rangle+\dot{x}_{2}(t)^{2}\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right\rangle+\dot{x}_{1}(t)\left\langle\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial_{t}\phi(t)\right\rangle\\
-\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial^{2}_{t}\phi(t)\right\rangle,

(83)

\ddot{x}_{2}(t)\Big(\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\langle\partial_{x}g(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right\rangle\Big)+\ddot{x}_{1}(t)\Big(\big\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\big\rangle\Big)=\dot{x}_{2}(t)^{2}\Big(\left\langle\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial_{x}g(t)\right\rangle\Big)\\
+\dot{x}_{2}(t)\Big(\left\langle\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial_{t}g(t)\right\rangle\Big)+\dot{x}_{1}(t)\dot{x}_{2}(t)\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right\rangle+(\dot{x}_{1}(t))^{2}\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle+\dot{x}_{2}(t)\left\langle\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial_{t}\phi(t)\right\rangle\\
-\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial^{2}_{t}\phi(t)\right\rangle.

(84)

Also, from the identity
g(t)=\phi(t)-H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}-H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1},
we obtain that

\partial_{t}g(t)=\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)+\dot{x}_{1}(t)\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+\dot{x}_{2}(t)\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1},

so, from the estimates (
66
) and (
81
), we obtain that

\left\lVert\partial_{t}g(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}=O(\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(85)

Now, since
\phi(t)
is a distributional solution of (
1
), we also have, from the global equality
\phi(t)=H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}+g(t),
the following identity

\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial^{2}_{t}\phi(t)\right\rangle=\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial^{2}_{x}g(t)-\ddot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right)g(t)\right\rangle-\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\left[\ddot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right)-\ddot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right)\right]g(t)\right\rangle\\
+\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\dot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right)+\dot{U}\left(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right)-\dot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right)\right\rangle\\
-\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\dot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}+g(t)\right)-\dot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right)-\ddot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right)g(t)\right\rangle

(86)

Since
\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\in kerD^{2}E_{pot}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right)
, we have by integration by parts that

\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial^{2}_{x}g(t)-\ddot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right)g(t)\right\rangle=0.

Since, we have

\dot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right)+\dot{U}\left(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right)-\dot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right)=24H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1})-6\sum_{j=1}^{4}\begin{pmatrix}5\\
j\end{pmatrix}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right)^{j}\left(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right)^{5-j},

(87)

Lemma
2.3
implies that

\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\dot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right)+\dot{U}\left(H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right)-\dot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right)\right\rangle=O\Big(e^{-\sqrt{2}(z(t))}\Big).

Also, we have from Taylor’s Expansion Theorem
the estimate

\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\dot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}+g(t)\right)-\dot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right)-\ddot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right)g(t)\right\rangle=O(\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{2}).

From Lemma
2.3
, the fact that
U
is a smooth function and
H_{0,1}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, we can obtain

\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\left[\ddot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right)-\ddot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right)\right]g(t)\right\rangle=O\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\left\lvert g(t)\right\rvert\,dx\Big)=O\Big(e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}z(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big).

In conclusion, we have

\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial^{2}_{t}\phi(t)\right\rangle=O\Big(\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{2}+e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}\Big),

(88)

and by similar arguments, we have

\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial^{2}_{t}\phi(t)\right\rangle=O\Big(\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{2}+e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}\Big).

(89)

Also, the equations (
83
) and (
84
) form a linear system with
\ddot{x}_{1}(t),\,\ddot{x}_{2}(t).
Recalling that the Matrix
M(t)
is uniformly positive, we obtain from the estimates (
75
), (
81
), (
85
), (
88
) and (
89
) that

\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\left\lvert\ddot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert=O(\epsilon).

(90)

∎

The Theorem
2.9
can also be improved when the kinetic energy of the solution is included in the computation and additional conditions are added, more precisely:

Theorem 2.10
.

\exists\delta_{0}>0,
such that if
0<\epsilon\leq\delta_{0},\,(\phi(0,x),\partial_{t}\phi(0,x))\in S\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
E_{total}((\phi(0,x),\partial_{t}\phi(0,x)))=2E_{pot}(H_{0,1})+\epsilon,

then there are
x_{2},\,x_{1}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that
g(t,x)=\phi(t,x)-H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)-H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}
satisfies

\left\langle g(t,x),\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)\right\rangle=0,\,\left\langle g(t,x),\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)\right\rangle=0,

and

\epsilon\cong e^{-\sqrt{2}(x_{2}(t)-x_{1}(t))}+\left\lVert(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t))\right\rVert_{H^{1},L^{2}}^{2}+\left\lvert\dot{x}_{1}(t)\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\dot{x}_{2}(t)\right\rvert^{2},

(91)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R},
which means the existence of positive constants
C,c
independent on
\epsilon
, such that for all
t\in\mathbb{R}

c\epsilon\leq e^{-\sqrt{2}(x_{2}(t)-x_{1}(t))}+\left\lVert(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t))\right\rVert_{H^{1},L^{2}}^{2}+|\dot{x}_{1}(t)|^{2}+|\dot{x}_{2}(t)|^{2}\leq C\epsilon.

(92)

Proof.

In this proof,
L^{2},\,H^{1}
mean, respectively,
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\,H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.
From Modulation Lemma and Theorem
2.9
, we can rewrite the solution
\phi(t)
in the form

\phi(t,x)=H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)+g(t,x)

with
x_{1}(t),\,x_{2}(t),\,g(t)
satisfying the conclusion of Theorem
2.9
. First we denote

\phi_{\sigma}(t)=\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x),-\dot{x}_{1}(t)\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}-\dot{x}_{2}(t)\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right)\in S\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

(93)

then we apply Taylor’s Expansion Theorem in
E(\phi(t))
around
\phi_{\sigma}(t)
, more precisely for
R_{\sigma}(t)
the residue of second order of Energy’s Taylor Expansion of
E(\phi(t),\partial_{t}\phi(t))
around
\phi_{\sigma}(t)
, we have:

2E_{pot}(H_{0,1})+\epsilon=E(\phi_{\sigma}(t))+\left\langle DE(\phi_{\sigma}(t)),\,\left(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t)\right)\right\rangle_{L^{2}\times L^{2}}+\frac{\left\langle D^{2}E(\phi_{\sigma}(t))\big(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t)\big),\,\big(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t)\big)\right\rangle_{L^{2}\times L^{2}}}{2}\\
+R_{\sigma}(t),

(94)

such that for
(w_{1},w_{2})\in S\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
(v_{1},v_{2})\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we have the identities

E(w_{1},w_{2})=\frac{\left\lVert\partial_{x}w_{1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\lVert w_{2}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}U(w_{1}(x))\,dx,

\displaystyle\left\langle DE(w_{1},w_{2}),(v_{1},v_{2})\right\rangle_{L^{2}\times L^{2}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}w_{1}(x)\partial_{x}v_{1}(x)+\dot{U}(w_{1})v_{1}+w_{2}(x)v_{2}(x)\,dx,

(95)

\displaystyle D^{2}E(w_{1},w_{2})=\begin{bmatrix}-\partial^{2}_{x}+\ddot{U}(w_{1})&0\\
0&\mathbb{I}\end{bmatrix}

(96)

with
D^{2}E(w_{1},w_{2})
defined as a bilinear operator from
H^{1}\times L^{2}
to
\mathbb{C}
. So, from identities (
95
) and (
96
), it is not difficult to verify that

R_{\sigma}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}U\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)+g(t,x)\right)-U\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)\right)-\dot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)\right)g(t,x)\\
-\frac{\ddot{U}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)\right)g(t,x)^{2}}{2}\,dx,

and, so,

\left\lvert R_{\sigma}(t)\right\rvert=O(\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{3}).

(97)

Also, we have

\left\langle DE(\phi_{\sigma}(t)),\,(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t))\right\rangle_{L^{2}\times L^{2}}=\left\langle DE_{pot}\left(H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}+H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right),\,g(t)\right\rangle+\left\langle-\dot{x}_{1}(t)\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}-\dot{x}_{2}(t)\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial_{t}g(t)\right\rangle.

(98)

The orthogonality conditions satisfied by
g(t)
also imply for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
that

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}g(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\dot{x}_{1}(t)\langle g(t),\,\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\rangle_{L^{2}},

(99)

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}g(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\dot{x}_{2}(t)\langle g(t),\,\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\rangle_{L^{2}}.

(100)

So, the inequality (
65
) and the identities (
98
), (
99
), (
100
) imply that

\left\lvert\langle DE(\phi_{\sigma}(t)),\,(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t))\rangle_{L^{2}\times L^{2}}\right\rvert=O\Big(\sup_{j\in\{1,2\}}\left\lvert\dot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert^{2}\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}+e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}\left\lVert g(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\Big).

(101)

From the Coercitivity Lemma and the definition of
D^{2}E(\phi_{\sigma}(t))
, we have that

\left\langle D^{2}E(\phi_{\sigma}(t))(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t)),\,(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t))\right\rangle_{L^{2}\times L^{2}}\cong\left\lVert(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t))\right\rVert_{H^{1}\times L^{2}}^{2}.

(102)

Finally, there is the identity

\left\lVert\dot{x}_{1}(t)\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}^{x_{1}(t)}(x)+\dot{x}_{2}(t)\partial_{x}H_{0,1}^{x_{2}(t)}(x)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=2\dot{x}_{1}(t)\dot{x}_{2}(t)\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{z(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\\
+\dot{x}_{1}(t)^{2}\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\dot{x}_{2}(t)^{2}\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(103)

From Lemma
2.3
, we have that
\left\lvert\langle\partial_{x}H^{z}_{0,1},\,\partial_{x}H_{-1,0}\rangle_{L^{2}}\right\rvert=O\big(ze^{-\sqrt{2}z}\big)
for
z
big enough.
Then, it is not difficult to verify that Lemma
2.4
, (
97
), (
101
), (
102
) and (
103
) imply directly the statement of the Theorem
2.10
which finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.11
.

Theorem
2.10
implies that it is possible to have a solution
\phi
of the equation (
1
) with energy excess
\epsilon>0
small enough satisfying all the hypotheses of Theorem
1.7
. More precisely, in notation of Theorem
1.7
, if
\left\lVert(g(0,x),\partial_{t}g(0,x))\right\rVert_{H^{1}\times L^{2}}\ll\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}},
then, from Theorem
2.10
, we have that

e^{-\sqrt{2}z(0)}+v_{1}^{2}+v_{2}^{2}\cong\epsilon.

In conclusion, we obtain that
E(\phi(0),\partial_{t}\phi(0))-2E_{pot}(H_{0,1})\cong\epsilon.

3
Long Time Behavior of Modulation Parameters

Even though Theorem
2.9
proves the orbital stability of a sum of two kinks with low energy excess, this theorem doesn’t explain the movement of the kinks’ centers
x_{2}(t),\,x_{1}(t)
and their speed for long time. More precisely, we still don’t know if there is a explicit smooth real function
d(t),
such that
(z(t),\dot{z}(t))
is close to
(d(t),\dot{d}(t))
in a large time interval.

But, the global estimates on the modulus of the first and second derivatives of
x_{1}(t),\,x_{2}(t)
obtained in Theorem
2.9
will be very useful to estimate with high precision the functions
x_{1}(t),\,x_{2}(t)
during a very large time interval. Moreover, we first have the following auxiliary lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
0<\theta,\,\gamma<1.
We recall the function

A(z)=E_{pot}(H^{z}_{0,1}+H_{-1,0})

for any
z>0.
If the same hypothesis of Theorem
2.9
are true and
let
\chi(x)
be a smooth function such that

\chi(x)=\begin{cases}1,\text{ if $x\leq\theta(1-\gamma)$,}\\
0,\text{ if $x\geq\theta$.}\end{cases}

(104)

and
0\leq\chi(x)\leq 1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}.
In notation of Theorem
2.9
, we denote

\chi_{0}(t,x)=\chi\Big(\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{z(t)}\Big),\,\overrightarrow{g(t)}=(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t))\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

and
\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert=\left\lVert(g(t),\partial_{t}g(t))\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

\alpha(t)=\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\dot{x}_{j}(t)^{2}z(t)e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}+\frac{\max_{j\in\{1,\,2\}}\dot{x}_{j}(t)^{2}}{z(t)\gamma}\Big(e^{-2\sqrt{2}z(t)(\frac{1-\gamma}{2-\gamma})}\Big)\\
+\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert\max_{j\in\{1,\,2\}}\left\lvert\dot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert\Big[1+\frac{1}{z(t)\gamma}+\frac{1}{z(t)^{2}\gamma^{2}}\max_{j\in\{1,\,2\}}\left\lvert\dot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert\Big]\Big(e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)(\frac{1-\gamma}{2-\gamma})}\Big)+\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert^{2}\Big[\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}z(t)^{2}}+\frac{1}{\gamma z(t)}+\Big(e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)(\frac{1-\gamma}{2-\gamma})}\Big)\Big].

(105)

Then, for
\theta=\frac{1-\gamma}{2-\gamma}
and the correction terms

\displaystyle p_{1}(t)=-\frac{\big\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)+\partial_{x}(\chi_{0}(t,x)g(t))\big\rangle}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}},

\displaystyle p_{2}(t)=-\frac{\big\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)+\partial_{x}([1-\chi_{0}(t,x)]g(t))\big\rangle}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}},

we have the estimates, for
j\in\{1,2\},

\left\lvert\dot{x}_{j}(t)-p_{j}(t)\right\rvert\lesssim\Big[1+\frac{\left\lVert\dot{\chi}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}}{z(t)}\Big]\Big(\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\left\lvert\dot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert+\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert^{2}\Big)+\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\left\lvert\dot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert z(t)e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)},

(106)

\left\lvert\dot{p}_{j}(t)+(-1)^{j}\frac{\dot{A}(z(t))}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}\right\rvert\lesssim\alpha(t).

(107)

Remark 3.2
.

We will take
\gamma=\frac{\ln{\ln{(\frac{1}{\epsilon})}}}{\ln{(\frac{1}{\epsilon})}}.
With this value of
\gamma
and the estimates of Theorem
2.9
, we will see in Lemma
5.1
that
\exists C>0
such that

\alpha(t)\lesssim\frac{\Big(\left\lVert(g_{0},g_{1})\right\rVert_{H^{1}\times L^{2}}+\epsilon\ln{(\frac{1}{\epsilon})}\Big)^{2}}{\ln{\ln{(\frac{1}{\epsilon})}}}\exp\Big(\frac{2C\left\lvert t\right\rvert\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\ln{(\frac{1}{\epsilon})}}\Big).

Proof.

For
\gamma\ll 1
enough and from the definition of
\chi(x),
it is not difficult to verify that

\left\lVert\dot{\chi}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\frac{1}{\gamma},\,\left\lVert\ddot{\chi}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}.

(108)

We will only do the proof of the estimates (
106
) and (
107
) for
j=1,
the proof for the case
j=2
is completely analogous. From the proof of Theorem
2.9
, we know that
\dot{x}_{1}(t),\,\dot{x}_{2}(t)
solve the linear system

M(t)\begin{bmatrix}\dot{x}_{1}(t)\\
\dot{x}_{2}(t)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}-\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\rangle\\
-\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\rangle\end{bmatrix},

where
M(t)
is the matrix defined by(
80
). Then, from Cramer’s rule, we obtain that

\dot{x}_{1}(t)=\frac{-\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle\Big(\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial_{x}g(t)\right\rangle+\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)}{\det(M(t))}+\frac{\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right\rangle\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle}{\det(M(t))}.

(109)

Using the definition (
80
) of the matrix
M(t)
,
\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert=O(\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}})
and Lemma
2.3
which implies the following estimate

\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1},\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle=O(z(t)e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}),

(110)

we obtain that

\left\lvert\det(M(t))-\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{4}\right\rvert=O\Big(\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert+z(t)^{2}e^{-2\sqrt{2}z(t)}\Big)=O(\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(111)

So, from the estimate (
111
) and the identity (
109
), we obtain that

\left\lvert\dot{x}_{1}(t)+\frac{1}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle\right\rvert=O\Big(\left\lvert\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial_{x}g(t)\right\rangle\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle\right\rvert\Big)\\
+O\Big(\left\lvert\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right\rangle\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right\rangle\right\rvert\Big)+O\Big(\left\lvert\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)\right\rangle\right\rvert\Big[\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert+z(t)^{2}e^{-2\sqrt{2}z(t)}\Big]).

(112)

Finally, from the definition of
g(t,x)
in Theorem
2.9
we know that

\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)=-\dot{x}_{1}(t)\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)-\dot{x}_{2}(t)\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}(x)+\partial_{t}g(t,x),

from the Modulation Lemma we also have verified that

\left\langle\partial_{t}g(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle=O\Big(\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert\left\lvert\dot{x}_{1}(t)\right\rvert\Big),\,\left\langle\partial_{t}g(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right\rangle=O\Big(\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert\left\lvert\dot{x}_{2}(t)\right\rvert\Big)

and from Theorem
2.9
we have that
\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert+\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}|\dot{x}_{j}(t)|\ll 1.
In conclusion, we can rewrite the estimate (
112
) as

\left\lvert\dot{x}_{1}(t)+\frac{1}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\right\rangle\right\rvert=O\Big(\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\left\lvert\dot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert+\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert^{2}+z(t)e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\left\lvert\dot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert\Big).

(113)

By a similar reasoning, we can also deduce that

\left\lvert\dot{x}_{2}(t)+\frac{1}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}H^{x_{2}(t)}_{0,1}\right\rangle\right\rvert=O\Big(\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\left\lvert\dot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert+\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert^{2}+z(t)e^{-\sqrt{2}z(t)}\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\left\lvert\dot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert\Big).

(114)

Following the reasoning of Lemma 3.5 of
[
19
]
, we will use the terms
p_{1}(t),\,p_{2}(t)
with the objective of obtaining the estimates (
107
), which have high precision and will be useful later to approximate
x_{j}(t),\,\dot{x}_{j}(t)
by explicit smooth functions during a long time interval.

First, it is not difficult to verify that

\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}(\chi_{0}(t)g(t))\right\rangle=O\Big(\Big[1+\frac{\left\lVert\dot{\chi}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}}{z(t)}\Big]\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert^{2}+\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\left\lvert\dot{x}_{j}(t)\right\rvert\left\lVert\overrightarrow{g(t)}\right\rVert\Big),

which clearly implies with estimate (
113
) the inequality (
106
) for
j=1.
The proof of inequality (
106
) for
j=2
is completely analog.

Now, the demonstration of the inequality (
107
) is similar to the proof of the second inequality of Lemma 3.5 of
[
19
]
. First, we have

\dot{p}_{1}(t)=-\frac{\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{t}\big(\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}(x)\big)\right\rangle}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}-\frac{\left\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}\big(\partial_{t}\chi_{0}(t)g(t)\big)\right\rangle}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}-\frac{\left\langle\partial_{x}\big(\chi_{0}(t)\partial_{t}g(t)\big),\,\partial_{t}\phi(t)\right\rangle}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}-\frac{\left\langle\partial_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0},\,\partial^{2}_{t}\phi(t)\right\rangle}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}\\
-\frac{\left\langle\partial_{x}\chi_{0}(t)g(t),\,\partial^{2}_{t}\phi(t)\right\rangle}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}-\frac{\left\langle\chi_{0}(t)\partial_{x}g(t),\,\partial^{2}_{t}\phi(t)\right\rangle}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}=I+II+III+IV+V+VI,

(115)

and we will estimate each term one by one. More precisely, from now on, we will work with a general cut function
\chi(x),
that is a smooth function
0\leq\chi\leq 1
satisfying

\chi(x)=\begin{cases}1,\text{ if $x\leq\theta(1-\gamma)$,}\\
0,\text{ if $x\geq\theta$.}\end{cases}

(116)

with
0<\theta,\,\gamma<1
and

\chi_{0}(t,x)=\chi\Big(\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{z(t)}\Big).

(117)

The reason for this notation is to improve the precision of the estimate of
\dot{p}_{1}(t)
by the searching of the
\gamma,\,\theta
which minimize
\alpha(t)
.

Step 1.
(Estimate of
I
)
We will only use the identity

I=\dot{x}_{1}(t)\frac{\big\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial^{2}_{x}H^{x_{1}(t)}_{-1,0}\big\rangle}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}.

Step 2.
(Estimate of
II.
)
We have, by chain rule and definition of
\chi_{0},
that

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle=-\frac{\big\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}\big(\partial_{t}\chi_{0}(t)g(t)\big)\big\rangle}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}=-\frac{\Big\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}\Big[\frac{d}{dt}\Big[\chi\Big(\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{z(t)}\Big)\Big]g(t,x)\Big]\Big\rangle}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Big\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}\Big(\dot{\chi}\Big(\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{z(t)}\Big)\frac{d}{dt}\Big[\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{z(t)}\Big]g(t)\Big)\Big\rangle}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}=\frac{\Big\langle\partial_{t}\phi(t),\,\partial_{x}\Big(\dot{\chi}\Big(\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{z(t)}\Big)\Big[\frac{\dot{x}_{1}(t)z(t)+(x-x_{1}(t))\dot{z}(t)}{z(t)^{2}}\Big]g(t)\Big)\Big\rangle}{\left\lVert\partial_{x}H_{0,1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}.

So, we obtain that
